#5018. Kalshi ends Donald Trump Jr. advisory role over Polymarket investment: Reuters

The prediction market operator said the decision was mutual and followed 1789 Capital’s investment in rival Polymarket, denying political considerations.

Prediction market platform Kalshi has ended its advisory relationship with Donald Trump Jr. after his venture capital firm increased its investment in rival Polymarket, Reuters reported Thursday.

A Kalshi spokesperson told Reuters that talks about ending Trump Jr.’s advisory role began in August, after Trump Jr.’s venture capital firm, 1789 Capital, took the lead on Polymarket’s $1 billion funding round. The round would value the company at $21 billion, with 1789 Capital set to contribute about $300 million.

“We have mutually agreed to end Don’s advisory role after his firm’s significant investment in a competitor this past summer,” the spokesperson said.

Trump Jr. joined Kalshi as a strategic adviser in January 2025 and received an equity stake reportedly worth $300,000 at the time, according to the Financial Times.

Kalshi’s spokesperson said political considerations played no role in the decision. Two people familiar with the matter told Reuters, however, that some within Kalshi had raised concerns about what Democrats’ midterm sweep of both chambers of Congress could mean for the company.

#5016. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, Nvidia shares plunged after co-founder Jensen Huang resigned as CEO and left the board. Coinbase’s head of EU policy said tighter DeFi rules could push users toward riskier platforms. Binance’s security chief warned about fake job interviews months before attackers used a similar tactic in the exchange’s $6.3 billion hack.

Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang resigned as president and CEO and stepped down from the company’s board on Thursday, effective immediately. The board named Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress interim CEO.

Kress will retain her role as CFO while Nvidia’s board considers internal and external candidates for the permanent CEO role. The board also appointed Harvey C. Jones its first chair and said it expects Nvidia’s operations to proceed without interruption.

Nvidia shares fell more than 13% shortly after the US market opened on Thursday, trading at $151.00 as of 2:31 p.m. UTC, according to Yahoo Finance. Bitcoin (BTC) fell alongside Nvidia shares, dropping as much as 3.5% to a multimonth low.

The shares later pared losses to trade at $165 at 4:24 p.m. UTC, down 5% on the day. The stock was down more than 26% over the previous month.

European Union efforts to tighten oversight of decentralized finance (DeFi) could drive crypto users toward platforms offering fewer investor protections, according to Coinbase’s head of EU policy.

Roeland Van der Stappen told Cointelegraph that regulated crypto platforms could respond by limiting or dropping access to DeFi services.

“Rules designed to protect investors would end up pushing activity towards the places with the least protection,” Van der Stappen said.

In its September response to the European Commission’s consultation on the Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation (MiCA), the European Securities and Markets Authority recommended creating a new category of regulated crypto services for firms that give customers access to DeFi protocols.

Months before Binance lost $6.3 billion to a hack, its chief security officer described to Cointelegraph how a fake job interview could give attackers a foothold inside the exchange — closely resembling one of the entry methods later used against the exchange.

During a July interview about Binance’s response to rising security threats, Jimmy Su told Cointelegraph that fake job interviews were “something we worry about a lot internally,” explaining how attackers could tailor a job opportunity to an employee before persuading them to run code as part of a technical assessment.

“And once that gets executed, the device is compromised and then they can start harvesting credentials,” Su said, adding that attackers could then use that to penetrate further into the network.

Su’s detailed warning about malicious coding assessments was not included in Cointelegraph’s July coverage, but takes on new significance following the biggest crypto exchange hack in history.

#5015. Why the Path to Quantum Resistance Runs Uphill for Bitcoin Wallet Makers

Why the Path to Quantum Resistance Runs Uphill for Bitcoin Wallet Makers

Bitcoin custody has always involved some degree of risk, but wallet manufacturers like Trezor—which build physical devices designed to keep a user’s private keys secure offline—are preparing for a post-quantum world where new challenges arise.

That’s because SHRINCS, the community’s leading candidate for a post-quantum signature scheme, allows users to make transactions using stateful signatures that require rigid state tracking to prevent funds from being stolen, according to Trezor CTO Tomáš Sušánka.

Proposed late last year by Blockstream Research Director Jonas Nick and cryptographer Mikhail Kudinov, SHRINCS centers on a composite hash-based approach: users can make transactions using either stateful signatures or stateless signatures.

Stateless signatures are quite large—potentially affecting fees, signature speed, and other efficiency metrics—but are safe to reuse, while smaller, more efficient stateful signatures come with serious security risks when reused beyond a particular signing budget.

The stateful component of SHRINCS is made up of a tree of one-time signatures, and if these keys are accidentally used more than once, enough cryptographic information is revealed for someone else to forge that user’s signature and effectively steal funds.

If Bitcoin successfully adopts SHRINCS, maintaining a meticulous count of the wallet’s state will become a critical responsibility for wallet developers—with lots of potential edge-case security risks.

“You’re giving them an option to mess up,” Sušánka said during a recent episode of MARA Foundation TV, in reference to state tracking. “It’s our job to make it in a secure way, right? It shouldn't be the user’s job.”

To mitigate this risk, Trezor is weighing options for how to support secure UX in a post-quantum world. For instance, Sušánka said the company is considering making the heavier, slower stateless signatures the default setting for all users, tucking the riskier stateful option behind an advanced settings wall.

While Sušánka recognizes that SHRINCS may be the conservative cryptographic choice because it relies on the same cryptographic assumptions as SHA-256, he is “kind of torn on the topic because maintaining the state is a burden.”

“For example, you can have two Trezors with the same recovery seed. How do you share the state between those two? You really don’t,” he added.

“That being said, as it is with everything, this is about trade-offs,” Sušánka continued. “The [stateful] signatures are 10 times smaller ... That makes a big difference. So, I just don't want to throw it off the table completely.”

Sušánka went on to share that while many are focused on Bitcoin’s migration path to post-quantum addresses and signatures, hardware wallet devices will also need to be physically updated for full quantum protection. Most signing devices use quantum-vulnerable cryptography for hardware-specific security functions, like firmware and device authentication.

If users want to ensure their devices are secure in a post-quantum world, they will need to use “quantum-ready” devices—that is, devices that use post-quantum cryptography for device and firmware authentication as well.

In anticipation of these needs, the company is already shipping products like Trezor Safe 7 that support post-quantum cryptography for wallet authentication processes. Of course, transactions made on these devices will only be quantum-secure once Bitcoin itself upgrades to post-quantum security.

That said, Sušánka warned developers not to wait too long to give users migration options, as he believes the migration of potentially millions of users will take time.

“People should be ready to make compromises,” Sušánka said. “If we’re all going to be very strict on what can and cannot be done, we’re not going to get anywhere.”

André Beganski is a Senior Content Manager at MARA Foundation, producing content on topics including the intersection of quantum computing and cryptography. He has previously worked for Decrypt.

#5012. NK hackers join memecoin trenches, xStocks adds Hong Kong equities: Asia Express

North Korean hackers launch memecoins to launder funds stolen in the Binance hack, while xStocks plans to add hundreds of Hong Kong-listed equities in the coming weeks.

North Korean hackers created memecoins to launder cryptocurrency stolen in the $6.3 billion Binance hack, according to a report from blockchain analytics firm Elliptic.Between Nov. 5 and 7, the hackers created at least six tokens on NEAR and used liquidity pools on decentralized exchanges to move $430,000 of stolen funds between wallets, Elliptic said. However, the tokens attracted almost no outside trading and were abandoned shortly after launch.

Elliptic said the same laundering technique was also used following the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in 2025, when North Korean-linked actors moved at least $24 million through a newly created token on the TRON blockchain.

Hong Kong’s wealthy investors are embracing crypto. (Chi Hung Wong)

Kraken parent Payward’s xStocks platform has added tokenized versions of more than 200 stocks listed in the United Kingdom and European Union.

Payward said the latest expansion brings xStocks’ offering to more than 1,500 tokenized assets. The company reported more than $45 billion in cumulative transaction volume and over 550,000 unique holders.

Payward said it plans to add hundreds of Hong Kong-listed equities in the coming weeks.

Deputy Secretary for Financial Services Chan Ho-lim told FinTech Week the region will introduces a new licensing regime for virtual-asset trading, custody, advisory and management, via amendment bills that will be submitted to Legislative Council this year.

Police have arrested 22 people in a cross border money laundering investigation, that allegedly involved a over the counter crypto network that moved more than HKD$150 million (US$18 million) while attempting to obscure the origin of funds. Meanwhile, the US has sanctioned 41 entities allegedly linked to the Cambodia’s Prince Group, including a man named Qiu Yong, who is accused of running Hong Kong companies on behalf of the criminal organization.

South Korea’s Financial Services Commission (FSC) plans to allow crypto exchanges to suspend deposits and withdrawals when hacking is suspected, rather than waiting until a breach is confirmed.

The FSC said it would issue a no-action letter permitting preventive suspensions under certain conditions. Current rules prohibit exchanges from blocking withdrawals without legitimate grounds and explicitly allow restrictions only when a hacking incident is clearly established.

The regulator also called for round-the-clock monitoring and incident response, higher cold-wallet holdings, and stronger insurance coverage and reserves.

South Korean crypto exchange GOPAX announced on November 6 it was suspending trading, deposits and withdrawals and moving all user assets to cold wallets. The firm had received threatening emails that claimed a hack was imminent, and took the drastic step of putting everything on ice on the recommendation of financial authorities.

Japanese financial services giant Nomura Holdings is reportedly weighing a sale of its cryptocurrency subsidiary Laser Digital as Nomura’s European operations continue to post losses.

Nomura has held talks with potential buyers, including Marex Group but is yet to make a final decision and could keep the business.

Nomura’s European businesses posted a pretax loss of 7.4 billion yen ($46.5 million) in the quarter ended Sept. 30, 2026, their fourth consecutive quarterly loss. The company posted pretax profits in every other reporting region over the same period.

Thailand is examining new rules for ETFs and ICO investors (Sasint)

Thailand’s securities regulator has proposed capping daily stablecoin deposits and withdrawals at 5 million baht ($148,000) each per customer at regulated crypto operators.

The limit would apply separately to deposits and withdrawals at each operator, Thailand’s Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) said in a Thursday announcement. The regulator said the rules aim to curb money laundering, cybercrime and attempts to bypass cross-border money transfer rules.

The draft would bar stablecoin transfers to and from third-party wallets.

Thailand’s SEC will block access to Bitget from December 5. It has also proposed new tests for retail investors before allowing them to invest in ICOs. The tests would require knowledge of at least six areas including digital tokens and investor rights, blockchain technology, digital-asset wallets and distribution channels. The proposals will be up for comment until November 25. The SEC is also seeking feedback on new rules around margin loans and ETFs, but has ruled out allowing leveraged or crypto ETFs at this stage.

South Korean crypto firm Bithumb Asset, has invested $85 million into a luxury resort project in Hoi An in Vietnam being built by local real estate developer Asia Estate. Da Nang police have issued a warning about scams via “ghost” cryptocurrency projects that are really just ponzi schemes in disguise. They highlighted a fraud ring involving the DRK cryptocurrency project, led by Huynh Duc Van, 33, and seven accomplices, which was recently dealt with by law enforcement.

Nomura’s former head of crypto derivatives trading, John Kim, has joined Chicago-based DV Trading in Singapore to lead a new discretionary crypto-trading team. A Singapore woman lost S$78,000 (US$60,391) in a four-month long crypto scam after someone posing as an insurance buyer “helped” her to set up a crypto wallet. She initially believed she was making money, before getting cleared out after she was directed to the little known Qevorinex platform. International bank communication network Swift argued at Singapore FinTech Festival for shared standards to prevent digital assets from fragmenting across incompatible systems.

#5010. Strategy Made $19.5 Billion in Profit. So Why Is MSCI Removing It From Stock Indexes?

Strategy Made $19.5 Billion in Profit. So Why Is MSCI Removing It From Stock Indexes?

Strategy made $19.53 billion in profit last quarter. Yet MSCI, one of the world’s biggest providers of stock indexes, has decided Michael Saylor’s Bitcoin company no longer belongs in some of its major investment benchmarks.

MSCI’s November review lists “STRATEGY A”—the company’s MSTR shares—for deletion from the MSCI USA Index after trading closes November 30. That also removes the stock from broader indexes that include U.S. companies, such as the MSCI World and MSCI All Country World indexes.

That sounds technical, but the distinction matters: MSCI is not removing Strategy from the Nasdaq stock exchange, shutting down its business, or making it sell Bitcoin. It is taking MSTR out of stock lists that investment funds use to decide which companies to own.

Consider an ETF designed to copy the MSCI USA Index. When MSTR disappears from the list, the fund generally has to sell its shares, regardless of whether the fund manager likes Saylor’s strategy.

This is why it’s bad news for Strategy. It means less indirect exposition to investments.

MSCI adopted new rules in October for so-called non-operating companies. The idea is to keep investment vehicles—businesses that mainly hold financial assets—out of indexes meant to track operating companies. Its screening process considers a company’s operating assets and other financial measures.

MSCI’s deletion list does not spell out exactly which tests Strategy failed. The company, however, argues that raising capital, managing its Bitcoin treasury and issuing financial products amount to a genuine operating business.

In an August letter to MSCI, Strategy called the proposal “discriminatory, arbitrary, and misguided.” It argued that MSCI should be a “mirror of the market, not an arbiter of it.”

Strategy also maintains a software business, but Bitcoin now dominates its finances. The disagreement is whether its Bitcoin activity counts as running a company or mainly managing an investment portfolio.

Strategy’s latest quarterly earnings show $19.53 billion in net income. But that does not mean its software customers paid the company anything close to that amount.

Nearly all of its $20.91 billion in reported operating income came from a rise in the accounting value of its Bitcoin. Under rules adopted in 2025, changes in Bitcoin’s market price appear in Strategy’s earnings even when it has not sold the coins. A decline in Bitcoin can produce the opposite result.

Its software division, meanwhile, recorded $125.4 million in quarterly revenue, down 3% from a year earlier. Its $477.2 million in revenue for all of 2025 was slightly below the $486.3 million reported in 2019, before Strategy began buying Bitcoin.

The immediate risk is selling pressure on MSTR, not on Bitcoin itself. Strategy estimated that funds linked to MSCI indexes hold roughly 13 million of its shares, or 3.1% of its outstanding common stock. Not all of that necessarily translates into sudden sales, and Strategy says the amount is manageable.

But Strategy has repeatedly raised money by selling shares and other securities, then used those proceeds to buy Bitcoin. If its stock falls, selling the same number of new shares brings in less cash.

So the company would have to issue more shares to raise the same amount—or buy fewer Bitcoin.

That is why a stock-index decision could eventually matter to the crypto market. The exclusion does not force a Bitcoin sale, and it does not guarantee fewer purchases. It could, however, weaken a key source of funding for one of Bitcoin’s biggest corporate buyers.

#5004. North Korea Stole $400 Million in Crypto Using Deepfake Video Calls, DOJ Says

North Korea Stole $400 Million in Crypto Using Deepfake Video Calls, DOJ Says

North Korean hackers have stolen more than $400 million in cryptocurrency over the past 18 months using a social-engineering scheme built on secretly recorded footage and AI deepfakes, the U.S. Justice Department said Thursday, as it announced the seizure of the infrastructure behind the operation.

The scheme, which the FBI calls “FaceStealer,” turns video conference calls into a trap. According to the DOJ, hackers lure targets into meetings using accounts stolen from previous victims, then play prerecorded webcam footage of trusted contacts to simulate their presence and win the target’s confidence.

The group also generates deepfake videos of well-known figures in the crypto and related industries, using AI to make the ruse more convincing.

Once a target is at ease, the attackers manufacture a fake technical glitch, such as claiming the person can’t be heard, and offer a link to “fix” it, which instead installs malware.

From there, the hackers can drain crypto wallets or exploit the victim’s access to other systems, sometimes waiting months or more than a year before striking. The FBI said it seized over 900 webcam recordings of FaceStealer victims under a federal warrant.

The DOJ tied the operation to North Korean groups known variously as Sapphire Sleet, BlueNoroff and APT38, among others, and warned the hackers have increasingly targeted open-source software developers in an apparent bid to enable supply-chain attacks.

The announcement came paired with an update on the record-breaking Binance hack. The department said it has now obtained court authorization to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion of the roughly $6.4 billion stolen from the exchange on Oct. 29, part of over $2.7 billion frozen so far.

The FBI attributed that theft to the North Korean group TraderTraitor, and the regime has been laundering the proceeds at speed, spinning up a new wallet roughly every 25 seconds. North Korea has denied involvement, calling the FBI’s finding a political provocation.

The actions underscore how central crypto theft has become to Pyongyang’s finances. The U.S. recently sanctioned 11 people over a North Korean IT-worker scheme that moved millions in crypto, and researchers have cataloged the many ways the regime’s hackers break into crypto firms.

Officials said efforts to trace and recover the stolen Binance funds are ongoing.
